        \\ \partial \bp{T_{tr}}_{\rho E} &= \frac{1}{\rho c_v^{*}}
    \end{align*}        

    \begin{align*}
        \partial \bp{p}_{\rho_s} &= R_s T_{tr} + \frac{\Rbar}{c_v^{*}} \bb{\frac{1}{\rho} \text{KE} - e_s^* - h_{f,s}}
        \\ \partial \bp{p}_{\rho u} &= -u \frac{\Rbar}{c_v^{*}}
        \\ \partial \bp{p}_{\rho v} &= -v \frac{\Rbar}{c_v^{*}}
        \\ \partial \bp{p}_{\rho w} &= -w \frac{\Rbar}{c_v^{*}}
        \\ \partial \bp{p}_{\rho E} &= \frac{\Rbar}{c_v^{*}}
    \end{align*}    
\end{multicols}


\subsection{Two-temperature models}

Two temperature models have the next level of robustness. The most famous is Park's two-temperature model, which makes the following assumptions:

\begin{enumerate}
    \item The translational and rotational temperatures of the heavy species are in equilibrium with one another: $\{T_t = T_r\} = T_{tr}$.
    \item All vibrational and electronic energies, along with the free-electron translational energy, are in equilibrium with one another: $\{T_{v} = T_{el} = T_e\} = T_{v}$.
\end{enumerate}

We can make further modelling choices to generate other 2T models. For example, by assuming the vibrational temperature is only evolved by the total vibrational energy and treating free-electron translational energy and electronic energies as functions of $T_{tr}$ we have another model. A more involved method splits the total vibrational energy up into one vibrational energy equation per polyatomic species. This gives one vibrational temperature for each polyatomic species. In every case, the electronic-electron energies are grouped together as $e_{el-e}$. Table [\ref{tab:2Tmodels}] shows which temperature evaluate which energy mode for certain models. The total energy equation remains the same as before by evaluating each energy mode with the correct temperature. 

\begin{table}[h!]
\centering
\begin{tabular}{lccc}
\toprule
\textbf{Model} & $\mathbf{e_{tr}}$ & $\mathbf{e_{v}}$ & $\mathbf{e_{el-e}}$ \\
\midrule
20 & $T_{tr}$ & $T_v$ & $T_{tr}$ \\
21 & $T_{tr}$ & $T_{v}$ & $T_{v}$ \\
22 & $T_{tr}$ & $T_{v,s}$ & $T_{tr}$ \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\caption{Temperatures used to evaluate each energy mode in the two-temperature models.}
\label{tab:2Tmodels}
\end{table}

\begin{equation*}
    \rho E = \sum_{s \in \mathcal{H}} + \rho_s e_{tr,s} (T_{tr}) + \rho_e e_{t,e} (T_{vee}) + \sum_{s \in \mathcal{P}} \rho_s e_{v,s} (T_{vee}) + \sum_{s \in \mathcal{H}} \rho_s e_{el,s} (T_{vee}) + \frac{1}{2} \rho \hat{u} \cdot \hat{u} + \sum_{s \in \mathcal{S}} \rho_s h_{f,s} 
\end{equation*}


The total vibrational-electronic-electron energy then:

\begin{equation}
    \rho E_{vee} = \sum_{s \in \polys} \rho_s e_{v,s} + \sum_{s \in \heavies} \rho_s e_{el,s} + \rho_e e_{tr,e}
\end{equation}

The pressure is then split up into contributions from the heavy species and electrons separately:

\begin{equation*}
    p = \sum_{s \in \heavies} \rho_s R_s T_{tr} + \rho_e R_e T_e
\end{equation*}

If $e_{el-e} (T_{tr})$ (models 20 and 22), this equation is just an expanded form of the normal sum over partial pressures. For $e_{el-e} (T_{v})$, (model 21), the free-electron translational temperature is not identical to the heavy-species one. Careful consideration must be taken when evaluating the pressure and its derivatives.


\begin{derivation}
    Another residual equation is needed to compute derivatives of $T_v$ from the vibrational energy equation. In the case that $e_{el-e}$ is evaluated at $T_v$, the residual is:

    \begin{equation}
        F = \sum_{s \in \polys} \rho_s e_{v,s} + \sum_{s \in \heavies} \rho_s e_{el,s} + \rho_e c_{v,e}^{tr} T_v - \rho E_{vee} = 0
    \end{equation}

    Derivatives are computed in the same as for $T_{tr}$. First, $\partial F / \partial T_v$ is found to be:

    \begin{equation*}
        \pd{F}{T_v} = \rho c_v^* = \sum_{s \in \polys} \rho_s c_{v,s}^v + \ub{ \sum_{s \in \heavies} \rho_s c_{v,s}^{el} + \rho_e c_{v,e}^{tr}}{\text{if included}}
    \end{equation*}
\end{derivation}


\begin{derivation}
    The derivative of pressure with respect to conserved variables is now more involved. Given:

    \begin{equation*}
        p = p_\mathcal{H} + p_e = \ub{\sum_{s \in \heavies} \rho_s R_s}{A} T_{tr} + \ub{\rho_e R_e}{B} T_e
    \end{equation*}

    The derivatives wrt conserved variables is then:

    \begin{equation*}
        \pd{p}{\boldsymbol{U}_j} = \pd{A}{\boldsymbol{U}_j} T_{tr} + A \pd{T_{tr}}{\boldsymbol{U}_j} + \pd{B}{\boldsymbol{U}_j} T_e + B \pd{T_e}{\boldsymbol{U}_j}
    \end{equation*}
\end{derivation}

\subsection{Three-temperature models}

Three-temperature models are the most involved models that will be gone over here. The assumptions are:

\begin{enumerate}
    \item The translational and rotational temperatures of the heavy species are in equilibrium with one another: $\{T_t = T_r\} = T_{tr}$.
    \item Vibrational energies are assumed to be in equilibrium at a single $T_v$ or in equilibrium with each molecular species' own $T_{v,s}$.
    \item Electronic energies, along with the free-electron translational energy, are in equilibrium with each other: $\{T_{el} = T_e\} = T_{e}$.
\end{enumerate}

For one vibrational temperature, the conserved variables are then:

\begin{align}
    \rho E = \sum_{s \in \mathcal{H}} + \rho_s e_{tr,s} (T_{tr}) + \rho_e e_{t,e} (T_{e}) + \sum_{s \in \mathcal{P}} \rho_s e_{v,s} (T_{v}) + \sum_{s \in \mathcal{H}} \rho_s e_{el,s} (T_{e}) + \frac{1}{2} \rho \hat{u} \cdot \hat{u} + \sum_{s \in \mathcal{S}} \rho_s h_{f,s} \\
    \rho E_v &= \sum_{s \in \polys} \rho_s e_{v,s} \\
    \rho E_{el-e} &=  \sum_{s \in \heavies} \rho_s e_{el-e} + \rho_e e_{tr,e}
\end{align}

For both three-temperature models, we can obtain $T_{tr}$ via a direct inversion. Both $T_{v,s}$ and $T_e$ need Newton methods. The derivatives are obtained in the same manner as before.

\newpage
\section{Nonequilibrium Transport Models}

High-enthalpy flows dissociate and ionize molecules and atoms. The mixture can no longer use a simple Sutherland's law for viscosity, or a constant Prandtl number for heat conductivity. Here, we go over different methods for computing the transport coefficients used in diffusive flux terms.

\subsection{Collision integral definitions}

\begin{definition}[Collision cross sections]
    The collision cross section has infinitely many values. Four of them are used for fluid dynamics to determine transport properties. The collision cross sections are, in general, the weighted averages of the cross sections for collisions between species $i$ and $j$ (sometimes denoted $s$ and $r$). They are defined as:
    
    \begin{equation}
        \pi \bar{\Omega}_{ij}^{(l,s)} = \frac{\int_{0}^{\infty} \int_{0}^{\pi} \exp(-\gamma^2) \gamma^{2s+3}(1 - \cos^l \chi) 4 \pi \sigma_{ij} \sin\chi d \chi d\gamma}{\int_{0}^{\infty} \int_{0}^{\pi} \exp(-\gamma^2) \gamma^{2s + 3} (1 - \cos^l{\chi}) \sin{\chi} d\chi d \gamma }
    \end{equation}

    Variables are:

    \begin{itemize}
        \item $\sigma_{ij} = \sigma_{ij}(\chi, g)$ is the differential-scattering cross section for the pair ($i,j$).
        \item $\chi$ is the scattering angle in the center of mass system.
        \item $g$ is the relative velocity of the colliding particles.
        \item $\gamma$ is the reduced velocity equal to $\displaystyle \bp{ \bb{\frac{m_i m_j}{2(m_i + m_j)kT}}^\frac{1}{2} }g$
    \end{itemize}

    In the collision integral symbol, $l$ controls how strong the contribution of the differential-scattering angle ($\chi$) is. $s$ is a weighting on the reduced velocity $(\gamma)$ of the particles. Of course, the subscript $(ii)$ refers to the collision integral for similar particles. Here is a list of where certain collision integrals show up:

    \begin{itemize}
        \item Viscosity ($\mu$) is a function of $\pi \bar{\Omega}_{ij}^{(2,2)}$ only.
        \item Translational component of thermal conductivity ($\kappa_\text{tr}$) is a function of $\pi \bar{\Omega}_{ij}^{(2,2)}$ only.
        \item The internal component of thermal conductivity ($\kappa_\text{int}$) is a function of $\pi \bar{\Omega}_{ij}^{(1,1)}$ only or a function of $\pi \bar{\Omega}_{ij}^{(1,1)}$ and $\pi \bar{\Omega}_{ij}^{(2,2)}$, depending on the expression used.
        \item The binary diffusion coefficient $D_{ij}$ is a function of $\pi \bar{\Omega}_{ij}^{(1,1)}$ only.
    \end{itemize}
\end{definition}


\begin{definition}[Gupta's curve fits for collision integrals]
    Gupta utilizes curve fits for the collision integrals. They have the following form:

    \begin{equation}
        \pi \bar{\Omega}_{ij}^{(l,s)} = \bb{\exp{D}} T^{\bb{A (\ln T)^2 + B \ln T + C}}
    \end{equation}

    \begin{equation}
        B_{i,j}^{*(2,2)} = \bb{\exp{C}} T^{\bb{A (\ln T)^2 + B \ln T}}
    \end{equation}


\end{definition}

\subsection{Transport properties of a single species}

\begin{definition}[Viscosity of a single species]

    The viscosity of a gas containing a single species is:  

    \begin{equation}
        \mu_i = \frac{5}{16} \frac{\sqrt{\pi m_i k T}}{\pi \bar{\Omega}_{ii}^{(2,2)}} = 8.3861 \cdot 10^{-6} \frac{\sqrt{\mathcal{M}_i T}}{\pi \bar{\Omega}_{ij}^{(2,2)}} \hspace{5mm} \text{units = } \frac{\text{N - s}}{\text{m}^2}
    \end{equation}

    Where $\mathcal{M}_i$ is the molecular weight of species $i$, and $T$ is the temperature in Kelvin. 

\end{definition}

\begin{definition}[Thermal conductivity of a single species]
    
    
    For the thermal conductivity of a single species, we have a number of contributions. The total $\kappa$ of the species is the sum of its internal energy specific contributions, i.e.:
    
    \begin{equation}
        \kappa = \kappa_\text{tr} + \kappa_\text{rot} + \kappa_\text{vib} + \kappa_\text{el} = \kappa_\text{tr} + \kappa_\text{int}
    \end{equation}

    Where the internal energies that are not translational have been grouped into $\kappa_\text{int}$. 

    \begin{equation}
        \kappa_{\text{tr}, i} = \frac{5}{2} \frac{\mu_i}{\mathcal{M}_i} (c_{v,i})_\text{tr} = \frac{15}{4} \frac{\hat{R}}{\mathcal{M}_i}\mu_i \hspace{5mm} \text{units = } \frac{\text{J}}{\text{m-s-K}}
    \end{equation}

    \begin{equation}
        \kappa_{\text{int}, i} = \frac{6}{5}\frac{c_{p_{\text{int},i}}}{\hat{R}} \frac{\hat{R}}{\mathcal{M}_i} \mu_i \frac{\pi \bar{\Omega}_{ii}^{(2,2)}}{\pi \bar{\Omega}_{ii}^{(1,1)}}
    \end{equation}

    Here, $c_{p_{\text{int},i}}$ is the total specific heat with the translation specific heat subtracted.

\end{definition}


\subsection{Transport properties of a mixture}

For species summation and Wilke's mixing rule, $\mu_j$ and $\kappa_j$ are calculated using the above equations for single species viscosity and conductivity.


\begin{definition}[Species summation]

    The concept of the species summation version of finding mixture transport properties is quite simple. It assumes no interaction between species and is expressed as:

    \begin{equation}
        \mu = \sum_{j=1}^\text{NS} X_j \mu_j \hspace{7.5mm} \kappa = \sum_{j=1}^\text{NS} X_j \kappa_j
    \end{equation}
    
\end{definition}



\begin{definition}[Wilke's mixing rule]

    Both viscosity and thermal conductivity can be approximated using Wilke's mixing rule. It uses a quantity, $\phi_i$, which is expressed as:

    \begin{equation}
        \phi_i = \sum_{j=1}^\text{NS} \frac{X_j \bb{1 + \sqrt{\frac{\mu_i}{\mu_j}} \bp{\frac{\mathcal{M}_j}{\mathcal{M}_i}}^\frac{1}{4}}^2}{\sqrt{8 \bp{1 + \frac{\mathcal{M}_i}{\mathcal{M}_j}}}} = \sum_{j=1}^\text{NS} \frac{X_j \bb{1 + \sqrt{\frac{\pi \bar{\Omega}_{jj}^{(2,2)}}{\pi \bar{\Omega}_{ii}^{(2,2)}}}}^2}{\sqrt{8 \bp{1 + \frac{\mathcal{M}_i}{\mathcal{M}_j}}}}
    \end{equation}

    Then the viscosity and thermal conductivity is found as:

    \begin{equation}
        \mu = \sum_{j=1}^\text{NS} \frac{X_j \mu_j}{\phi_j} \hspace{5mm} \kappa = \sum_{j=1}^\text{NS} \frac{X_j \kappa_j}{\phi_j}
    \end{equation}    
\end{definition}


\begin{definition}[Chapman-Enskog approximation method]
    For obtaining mixture transport quantities using Chapman-Enskog approximations, a number of new variables are used. This is the most rigorous method for finding the properties. The units of $\Delta$ is in centimeters-second, while $\alpha_{ij}$ is unitless.

    \begin{equation}
        \Delta_{ij}^{(1)} = \frac{8}{3} (1.5460 \cdot 10^{-20}) \bb{\frac{2 \mathcal{M}_i \mathcal{M}_j}{\pi N_A k T (\mathcal{M}_i + \mathcal{M}_j)}}^\frac{1}{2} \pi \bar{\Omega}_{ij}^{(1,1)}
    \end{equation}

    \begin{equation}
        \Delta_{ij}^{(2)} = \frac{16}{5} (1.5460 \cdot 10^{-20}) \bb{\frac{2 \mathcal{M}_i \mathcal{M}_j}{\pi N_A k T (\mathcal{M}_i + \mathcal{M}_j)}}^\frac{1}{2}  \pi \bar{\Omega}_{ij}^{(2,2)}
    \end{equation}

    For electron-electron collisions, we have:

    \begin{equation}
        \pi \bar{\Omega}_{ee}^{(2,2)} = 1.29 Q_e \cdot 10^{16}
    \end{equation}

    For ion-ion collisions, we have:

    \begin{equation}
        \pi \bar{\Omega}_{II}^{(2,2)} = 1.36 Z^4 Q_e \cdot 10^{16}
    \end{equation}

    Then for both:

    \begin{equation}
        \pi \bar{\Omega}_{ee}^{(1,1)} = \pi \bar{\Omega}_{II}^{(1,1)} = 0.795 Z^4 Q_e \cdot 10^{16}
    \end{equation}

    Here, $Z=1$ for singly ionized species (for aerospace applications this is fine), and:

    \begin{equation}
        Q_e = \frac{e^4}{(kT)^2} \ln \Lambda \hspace{5mm} \text{cm}^2
    \end{equation}

    \begin{align}
        \Lambda &= \bb{\frac{9 (kT)^3}{4 \pi e^6 n_e} + \frac{16 (kT)^2}{e^4 n_e^{2/3}}}^{1/2}
        \\ &= \bb{2.09 \cdot 10^{-2} \bp{\frac{T^4}{10^{12} p_e}} + 1.52 \bp{\frac{T^4}{10^{12}p_e}}^{2/3}}^{1/2}
    \end{align}

    Here, $p_e$ is the electron pressure in atmospheres, $n_e$ is the number density of electrons, and $e = 4.8 \cdot 10^{-10}$  esu. These equations are only useful unter for $p_e < $ some limiting value, which should be true in aerospace applications:

    \begin{equation}
        p_{em} = 0.0975 \bp{\frac{T}{10^3}}^4
    \end{equation}

    Depending on the data given, the Coulomb cross section may need to be altered. Gupta and Yos give collision cross sections at the above limiting electron pressure, so at any other pressure the cross section needs to be changed:

    \begin{align}
        \frac{\pi \bar{\Omega}_{ij}^{(l,s)}(p_e)}{\pi \bar{\Omega}_{ij}^{(l,s)}(p_{em})} &= \ln \Lambda (p_e)
        \\ &= \frac{1}{2} \ln \bb{2.09 \cdot 10^{-2} \bp{\frac{T}{1000 p_e^{1/4}}}^4 + 1.52 \bp{\frac{T}{1000 p_e^{1/4}}}^{8/3}}
    \end{align}

    Finally, $\alpha_{ij}$ is needed for the heavy-particle translational component of thermal conductivity:

    \begin{equation}
        \alpha_{ij} = 1 + \frac{[1 - (\mathcal{M}_i / \mathcal{M}_j)][0.45 - 2.54(\mathcal{M}_i / \mathcal{M}_j)]}{[1 + (\mathcal{M}_i / \mathcal{M}_j)]^2}
    \end{equation}

    Assuming thermochemical nonequilibrium, the mixture viscosity \textit{appears} to not be a function of vibrational or rotation energy, and is thus found with:

    \begin{equation}
        \mu = \sum_{i=1}^{\text{NS} - 1} \bb{\frac{\frac{\mathcal{M}_i}{N_A}X_i}{\sum_{j=1}^\text{NS - 1} X_j \Delta_{ij}^{(2)}(T) + X_e \Delta_{ie}^{(2)}(T_e) }} + \frac{\frac{\mathcal{M}_e}{N_A} X_e }{\sum_{j=1}^\text{NS} X_j \Delta_{ej}^{(2)}(T_e)}
    \end{equation}

    Where $X_j$ is the molar fraction, $T$ is the Temperature, and $T_e$ is the electron temperature. In constrast to viscosity, the thermal conductivity is dependant on all energy modes, and therefore has contributions from each mode. They are:
    \vspace{5mm}

    Translational component without electron-heavy-particle and electron-electron collision:

    \begin{equation}
        K_\text{tr}^* = (2.3901 \cdot 10^{-8}) \frac{15}{4} k \sum_{i=1}^\text{NS-1} \bb{\frac{X_i}{\sum_{j=1} \alpha_{ij} \Delta_{ij}^{(2)}(T) + 3.54 X_e \Delta_{ie}^{(2)}(T_e) }}
    \end{equation}

    Rotational component. "p.e." means  "partial excitation" while "f.e." mean "full excitation". If it is in equilibrium with translational temperature, $T_\text{rot} = T$:

    \begin{equation}
        (K_\text{rot})_\text{p.e.} = (2.3901 \cdot 10^{-8})k \sum_\text{i=mol.} \bb{\frac{\bb{\frac{c_{p,i}(T_\text{rot})}{\hat{R}} - \frac{5}{2}} X_i}{\sum_{j=1}^\text{NS-1}X_j \Delta_{ij}^{(1)}(T) + X_e \Delta_{ie}^{(1)}(T_e)}}
    \end{equation}

    \begin{equation}
        (K_\text{rot})_\text{f.e.} = (2.3901 \cdot 10^{-8})k \sum_\text{i=mol.} \bb{\frac{X_i}{\sum_{j=1}^\text{NS-1}X_j \Delta_{ij}^{(1)}(T) + X_e \Delta_{ie}^{(1)}(T_e)}}
    \end{equation}


    Vibrational component:

    \begin{equation}
        (K_\text{vib})_\text{p.e.} = (2.3901 \cdot 10^{-8})k \sum_\text{i=mol.} \bb{\frac{\bb{\frac{c_{p,i}(T_\text{vib})}{\hat{R}} - \frac{7}{2}} X_i}{\sum_{j=1}^\text{NS-1}X_j \Delta_{ij}^{(1)}(T) + X_e \Delta_{ie}^{(1)}(T_e)}}
    \end{equation}

    \begin{equation}
        (K_\text{vib})_\text{f.e.} = (2.3901 \cdot 10^{-8})k \sum_\text{i=mol.} \bb{\frac{X_i}{\sum_{j=1}^\text{NS-1}X_j \Delta_{ij}^{(1)}(T) + X_e \Delta_{ie}^{(1)}(T_e)}}
    \end{equation}

    Electronic component:

    \begin{equation}
        K_\text{el} = (2.3901 \cdot 10^{-8})k \sum_\text{i=1}^\text{NS-1} \bb{\frac{\bb{\frac{c_{p,i}(T_\text{el})}{\hat{R}} - \frac{9}{2}} X_i}{\sum_{j=1}^\text{NS-1}X_j \Delta_{ij}^{(1)}(T) + X_e \Delta_{ie}^{(1)}(T_e)}}
    \end{equation}

    Free electron component:

    \begin{equation}
        K_\text{e} = (2.3901 \cdot 10^{-8})k \frac{X_e}{\sum_{j=1}^\text{NS-1}X_j \Delta_{ej}^{(2)}(T_e) + X_e \Delta_{ee}^{(2)}(T_e)}
    \end{equation}




\end{definition}



\newpage

\newpage

\section{Nonequilibrium Source Terms}

\subsection{Species Mass Source Term}
\label{sec:chem_source}


For a multicomponent gas with NS reacting species and NR different chemical reactions, the stoichiometric relations for the change in reactants and products are:

\begin{equation}
    \sum_{i=1}^\text{NS} \nu_i' X_i \leftrightarrows \sum_{i=1}^\text{NS} \nu_i'' X_i, 
    \hspace{5mm} r=1, \dots , \text{NR}
\end{equation}

Where $\nu_i'$ and $\nu_i''$ are the stoichiometric coefficients for reactants and products respectively. $k_{f,r}$ is the rate coefficient that governs the forward reaction (right arrow), while $k_{b,r}$ governs the backward reaction (left arrow). The net rate of mass production of a species per unit volume has a number of different expressions. Consider the reaction:

\begin{equation}
    O_2 + M \longrightarrow 2O + M
    \label{eq:reaction-example-1}
\end{equation} 

Here, M is an arbitrary collision partner, deemed a \textit{third-body}. Concentration of species $j$ is the moles of $j$ per unit volume and is denoted using brackets, hence $[O_2]$ is the molar concentration of $O_2$. The time rate of change of the concentration of $O$ in Eq.[\ref{eq:reaction-example-1}] is then:

\begin{equation}
    \frac{d [O]}{dt} = 2k [O_2][M]
\end{equation}

Where $k$ is the reaction rate. This equation gives the rate at which Eq. [\ref{eq:reaction-example-1}] goes from left to right. Therefore, $k$ is actually the \textit{forward} reaction rate, $k_f$. The two previous equations should then be written as:

\begin{equation}    
    O_2 + M \overset{k_f}{\longrightarrow} 2O + M
\end{equation} 

\begin{equation}
    \frac{d [O]}{dt} = 2k_f [O_2][M]
\end{equation}

Of course, the right to left reaction is the \textit{backwards} reaction and is governed by the backwards rate coefficient:

\begin{equation}
    O_2 + M \underset{k_b}{\longleftarrow} 2O + M
\end{equation}

\begin{equation}
    \frac{d [O]}{dt} = -2 k_b [O]^2[M]
\end{equation}

You must combine these forward and backwards equations because both reactions happen simultaneously. This gives the following governing equations for mass production in this reaction:

\begin{equation}
    O_2 + M \overset{k_f}{\underset{k_b}{\longleftrightarrow}} 2O + M
\end{equation}

\begin{equation}
    \frac{d [O]}{dt} = 2 \bp{k_f [O_2][M] - k_b [O]^2[M]}
\end{equation}

The net rate equation for an arbitrary reaction is then:

\begin{equation}
    \frac{d [X_i]}{dt} = (\nu_i'' - \nu_i') \left \{ k_f \prod_i [X_i]^{\nu_i'} - k_b \prod_i [X_i]^{\nu_i''} \right \}
\end{equation}

Where $\nu'$ is the stoichiometric for a reactant species, and $\nu''$ is for product species. The equilibrium constant is very useful to find one reaction rate from the other. There are two equilibrium constants: $K_c$ is the equilibrium constant based on concentrations, and $K_p$ is the equilibrium constant based on partial pressures. They are related through:

\begin{equation}
    K_{c,r} = \bp{\frac{p_\circ}{\hat{R}T}}^{\nu_{m,r}} \exp{\bb{- \sum_s (\nu_{s,r}'' - \nu_{s,r}') \bp{\frac{H_s^\circ}{\hat{R}T} - \frac{S_s^\circ}{\hat{R}}}}}
\end{equation}

Where it is raised to a sum due to the complicated nature of the equilibrium constant. Typically, the rate coefficient has variable units, since it depends on the number of molecules in the reaction, which gives rise to a key concept:

\begin{remark}
For most chemical kinetic models used for modelling air, the reactants usually consist of two species, while the products may contain two or three species. Therefore, the forward rate coefficients $k_f$ should always have the same units. Since the equilibrium constant is used to find $k_b$, no unit conversions are necessary besides perhaps from cgs to SI units.
\end{remark}

With the equilibrium constant of a reaction known, the forward/backward reaction rates can be found via the following relation:

\begin{equation}
    \frac{k_{f,r}(T)}{k_{b,r}(T)} = K_\text{c,r}
    \label{eq:rate-relationship}
\end{equation}

Both forward and backward rate coefficients can be found using the Arrhenius or modified Arrhenius equations, though usually only the forward rate is computed and the backward rate is found from Eq. [\ref{eq:rate-relationship}]. The Arrhenius equation has the form:

\begin{equation}
    k_{f,r} = C e^{-E_a/\kB T}
\end{equation}

Where C is a constant, $E_a$ is the activation energy, and $T$ is the temperature. The modified Arrhenius form (more widely used) is:

\begin{equation}
    k_{f,r} = C T^\eta e^{-E_a/ \kB T}
\end{equation}

Here, $\eta$ is a reaction specific exponent, and $E_a$ depends on the reaction (could be a dissociation energy, could be 0 for recombination, etc.)


\subsection{Reaction Types}

\begin{definition}[Dissociation and recombination]

    A dissociation reaction occurs when a molecule collides with a particle and the collision energy is large enough to break the molecular bonds between one or more atoms in the molecule. This causes the molecule to dissociate. Recombination is just the reverse of dissociation. Examples of these reactions are:

    \begin{subequations}
    \begin{align}
        O_2 + M &\leftrightarrows 2O + M
        \\ N_2 + M &\leftrightarrows 2N + M
        \\ NO + M &\leftrightarrows N + O + M
        \\ O_2 + N &\leftrightarrows NO + O 
        \label{shuffle}
        \\ N_2 + O &\leftrightarrows NO + N
        \\ N_2 + O_2 &\leftrightarrows 2NO
        \\ N + O &\leftrightarrows NO^+ + e^- 
        \label{dissrecomb}
    \end{align}
    \end{subequations}

    Eq. [\ref{shuffle}] is an example of a \textit{bimolecular exchange} reaction. Eq. [\ref{dissrecomb}] is called a \textit{dissociative-recombination}  reaction since the recombination of the $NO^+$ ion with an electron produces a dissociated product.
    
\end{definition}

\begin{definition}[Associative ionization]
    An associate ionization reaction is a reaction where two neutral atoms combine into an ionic molecule, releasing an electron in the process. Examples are:

    \begin{subequations}
    \begin{align}
        N + O &\leftrightarrows NO^+ + e^- \\
        O + O &\leftrightarrows O_2^+ + e^- \\
        N + N &\leftrightarrows N_2^+ + e^- 
    \end{align}
    \end{subequations}
\end{definition}

\begin{definition}[Charge exchange] 
    A charge exchange reaction occurs when particles exchange an electron during a collision. An example of this is:

    \begin{equation}
        O^+ + N_2 \leftrightarrows N_2^+ + O
    \end{equation}
\end{definition}

\begin{definition}[Electron-impact ionization]
    An electron-impact ionization reaction happens when a neutral particle is ionized by an electron. Examples are:

    \begin{equation}
        \begin{aligned}
        N + e^- &= N^+ + e^- + e^- \\
        O + e^- &= O^+ + e^- + e^-
        \end{aligned}
        \label{def:el-imp-ion-reac}
    \end{equation}    
\end{definition}

\begin{definition}[Radiative recombination]
    Needs addition
\end{definition}

\subsection{Efficiencies in Third-Body Reactions}
For certain reactions like dissociation, the outcome of the reaction is the same regardless of the other reactant. These are called \textit{third-body reactions}, since the secondary reactant does not change chemically. The third-body is usually represented with an $M$ as we have already discussed:

\begin{equation}
    N_2 + M \rightleftarrows N + N + M \label{thirdbody}
\end{equation}

This $M$ can be any species, and the Arrhenius equation is nearly identical for any $M$, with only a slight change in the leading ceofficient $C$. This coefficient change is called an \textit{efficiency} (denoted here as $\epsilon$), and the algebra will be shown as to how this is beneficial to use. A list of efficiencies in certain reactions are shown in Table \ref{efficiencies}.

\begin{center}
\captionof{table}{Change in modified Ahhrenius constant $C$ for some third-bodies in $N_2$ dissociation.}
\label{efficiencies}
\begin{tabular}{p{25mm} p{15mm} p{15mm}}
\hline
Third body & C & $\epsilon$ \\
\hline
$M = N_2$ & 7.0e21 & 1.0 \\
$M = O_2$ & 7.0e21 & 1.0 \\
$M = NO$  & 7.0e21 & 1.0 \\
$M = N$   & 3.0e22 & 4.2857 \\
$M = O$   & 3.0e22 & 4.2857 \\
\hline
\end{tabular}
\end{center}

Since $7.0e21$ is the smallest value, this is given an efficiency of 1.0, and then any other reaction's efficiency is given as some multiplier of this base value. The physical meaning of efficiencies is how "efficient" that third body is at making that reaction happen. $\epsilon$ is larger for atoms, since they are more efficient in sending their energy into dissociation of a molecule. We can now do some algebra on the Arrhenius equation by letting:

\begin{equation}
    C = \epsilon C_c
\end{equation}

\begin{equation}
    \gamma = C_c T^\eta e^{E_0/kT}
\end{equation}

The forward and backwards reaction constants are then:

\begin{equation}
    k_f = \epsilon \gamma, \hspace{5mm} k_b = \frac{\epsilon \gamma}{K_{eq}}
\end{equation}

For a third body reaction, the change in the concentration of $N_2$ due to a single third-body $M$ is then:

\begin{align*}
    \frac{d[N_2]}{dt} &= (\nu'' - \nu') \left\{ \epsilon \gamma [N_2][M] - \frac{\epsilon \gamma}{K_{eq}}[N]^2[M] \right\} 
    \\ &= (\nu'' - \nu') \cdot \epsilon \cdot [M] \underbracket{ \left\{ \gamma [N_2] - \frac{\gamma}{K_{eq}}[N]^2 \right\}}_{R}
\end{align*}

$R$ is independant of the third-body, so it is a constant and only needs to be computed once. Summing over contributions from all independant reactions gives:

\begin{align*}
    \frac{d[N_2]}{dt} &= \underbracket{(0 - 1)}_{\nu_{N_2}'' - \nu_{N_2}'} \bcb{\sum_{i} \epsilon_i \cdot [M_i]} R
\end{align*}

Where:

\begin{equation}
    \sum_i \epsilon_i [M_i] = \epsilon_{N_2} [N_2] + \epsilon_{O_2} [O_2] + \epsilon_{NO} [NO] + \epsilon_{N} [N] + \epsilon_{O} [O] + \dots
\end{equation}

$R$ is retained for the production of $N$ atoms as well, you only need to change the coefficient from the $\nu_i'' - \nu_i''$ term:

\begin{align*}
    \frac{d[N]}{dt} = \ub{(2 - 0)}{\nu_{N}'' - \nu_N'} \bcb{\sum_i \epsilon_i \cdot [M_i]} R
\end{align*}



\subsection{Example rate equation solution}

    Here, and example is done to find the rate of production of $NO$. Consider the following reactions that all contain $NO$:

    \begin{subequations}
        \begin{align}
            NO + O_2 &\leftrightarrows N + O + O_2
            \\ NO + N_2 &\leftrightarrows N + O + N_2
            \\ NO + NO &\leftrightarrows N + O + NO
            \\ NO + O &\leftrightarrows N + O + O
            \\ NO + N &\leftrightarrows N + O + N
            \\ O_2 + N &\leftrightarrows NO + O
            \\ N_2 + O &\leftrightarrows NO + N
        \end{align}
    \end{subequations}

    The forward and backward rate coefficients for the reactions are $k_{f,a}$ and $k_{b,a}$ for the first reaction, all the way down with consistent lettering with the equation number. The entire equation is then:
    
    \begin{align*}
        \frac{d[NO]}{dt} &= -\bcb{\gamma [NO] - \frac{\gamma}{K_{eq}} [N][O]} \sum_i \epsilon_i [M]
        \\ &+ \bcb{k_{f,f} [O_2][N] - k_{b,f} [NO][O]}
        \\ &+ \bcb{k_{f,g} [N_2][O] - k_{b,g} [NO][N] }
    \end{align*}
    

\newpage

\subsection{Translational-Vibrational Energy Relaxation}
\label{sec:vib-trans-relax}

The energy exchange between the vibrational and translational energy modes is a source term that needs to be modelled in both two- or three-temperature systems of conservation equations. It is expressed as the sum of the energy exchange between each species containing a vibrational energy mode:

\begin{equation}
    \mathcal{S}_{t-v}^v = \sum_s^\text{ND} \mathcal{S}_{t-v,s} = \sum_s^\text{ND} \rho_s \frac{e_{vs}(T_{tr}) - e_{vs}(T_v)}{<\tau_s>}
    \label{eq:vib-relax}
\end{equation}

In Eq. [\ref{eq:vib-relax}], $e_{vs}(T_{tr})$ is the equilibrium vibrational energy evaluated at the trans-rotational tempertature, $T_{tr}$, while $e_{vs}(T_v)$ is the actual vibrational energy evaluated with $T_v$. The molar-averaged relaxation time, $<\tau_s>$ is defined by:

\begin{equation*}    
    <\tau_s> = \frac{\sum_r \Xmol_r}{\sum_r \Xmol_r / \tau_{sr}^\text{MW}}
\end{equation*}

This sum takes placeover all particles $r$ (atomic or polyatomic), and molecules $s$. The term $\tau_{sr}$ is the relaxation time between the molecule $s$ and particle $r$ and is often computed using the Millikan-White curve-fits:

\begin{equation*}
    \tau_{sr}^\text{MW} = \frac{101325}{p} \exp{[A_{sr} (T_{tr}^{-\frac{1}{3}} - B_{sr}) - 18.42]}
\end{equation*}

Where $A_{sr}$ and $B_{sr}$ are either set constants or computed from the following definitions:

\begin{align*}
    \mu_{sr} &= \frac{\MW_s \MW_r}{\MW_s + \MW_r}
    \\ A_{sr} &= 1.16 \times 10^{-3} \mu_{sr}^{1/2} \theta_{vs}^{4/3}
    \\ B_{sr} &= 0.015 \mu_{sr}^{1/4}
\end{align*}

The Park high temperature correction is added to the molar averaged Millikan-White time:

\begin{equation*}
    \tau_s = <\tau_s> + \tau_s^P
\end{equation*}

Where $\tau_s^P$ is defined as:

\begin{align*}
    \tau_{s}^\text{P} &= \frac{1}{\bar{c}_s \sigma_s n}
    \\ \bar{c}_s &= \sqrt{\frac{8 \Rhat T_{tr}}{ \MW_s \pi}}
    \\ \sigma_s &=  \sigma_{v,s} \bp{\frac{50000}{T_{tr}}}^2
    \\ n &= \frac{p}{\kB T_{tr}}
\end{align*}

Here, $\sigma_{v,s}$ is a constant for each species.

\subsection{Vibrational-Vibrational Energy Relaxation} 
\label{sec:vib-vib-relax}

When modelling vibrational energy, sometimes the vibrational energy of each polyatomic species is modelled with a separate conservation equation. In this case, the energy exchange between vibrational modes of different species needs to be modelled. Candler \cite{candlerthesis} states the vibrational energy transfer between species $s$ and $r$ is given by by product of a probability, $P_{s-r}$, a number of $s-r$ collisions per unit volume and time, $Z_{s-r}$, and the average vibrational energy per species $r$:

\begin{equation*}
    \mathcal{S}_{v-v,s,r} = P_{sr} Z_{s-r} \epsilon_{v,r}
\end{equation*}

The number of collisions per unit volume and time is given from kinetic theory as:

\begin{equation*}
    Z_{s-r} = N_s N_r \sigma_{sr} \sqrt{\frac{8 \kB T_t}{\pi \mu_{sr}}}
\end{equation*}

Here, $\mu_{sr}$ is the reduced mass of species $s$ and $r$, and $\sigma_{sr}$ is the collision cross section between species $s$ and $r$. The average vibrational energy of species $r$ is easily taken from the vibrational energy equation:

\begin{equation*}
    \epsilon_{v,r} = \frac{\Rhat}{\NA} \frac{\theta_{v,r}}{e^{\theta_{v,r}/T_{v,r}} - 1} = e_{v,r} \MW_r = \frac{E_{v,r} \MW_r}{\rho_r} 
\end{equation*}

Finally, the probabability of energy transfer between species $s$ and $r$ is taken as $10^{-2}$. For species $s$, the vibrational energy transfer is then:

\begin{equation*}
    \mathcal{S}_{v-v,s} = \sum_{r \neq s}^\text{ND} \bb{\NA \sigma_{sr} \sqrt{\frac{8 \kB T_t}{\pi \mu_{sr}}} \bp{P_{s-r} \frac{\rho_s}{\MW_s}E_{v,r} - P_{r-s} \frac{\rho_r}{\MW_r}E_{v,s}}} 
\end{equation*}


\subsection{Electron-Translational Energy Relaxation}
\label{sec:el-trans-relax}
The transfer of energy between electron and translational energy modes is a source term that needs to be modelled in either a two-temperature vibration-electronic-electron or a three-temperature electronic-electron conservation equation. This source term is given by:

\begin{equation*}
    \mathcal{S}_{t-e}^e = 3 \rho_e  \Rhat (T_{tr} - T_e) \sum_{s \neq e}^\text{NS} \frac{\nu_{es}}{\MW_s} \hspace{5mm} \text{Ref. \cite{gnoffo1989}}
\end{equation*}

Note that this is a Landau-Teller like form for energy relaxation:

\begin{equation*}
    \mathcal{S}_{t-e}^e = \frac{e_e(T_{tr}) - e_e(T_e)}{<\tau_{es}>} \hspace{5mm} \text{with} \hspace{5mm} \frac{1}{<\tau_{es}>} = \sum_{s \neq e} ^\text{NS} \frac{\nu_{es}}{\MW_s}
\end{equation*}

The frequency $\nu_{es}$ for electron-ion collisions is computed as:

\begin{equation*}
    \nu_{es} = \frac{8}{3} \sqrt{\frac{\pi}{m_e}} n_s \frac{q^2}{(2 \kB T_e)^\frac{3}{2}} \ln \bb{\frac{(\kB T_e)^3}{\pi n_e q^3}}
\end{equation*}

Where we have the following equation for $q$, with $\elq$ measured in Coulombs. Note that many other equations are given in CGS units. As such, in those representations, $\elq$ is measured in esu and the denominator is set to one. As stated, we use $\elq$ as measured in Coulumbs, and all other variables in SI units.

\begin{equation*}
    q = \frac{\elq^2}{4 \pi \epso}
\end{equation*}

The collision frequency for electron-neutral combinations is:

\begin{equation*}
    \nu_{es} = n_s \sigma_{es} \sqrt{\frac{8 \kB T_e}{\pi m_e}}
\end{equation*}

Where the cross section $\sigma_{es}$ is taken from curve fits:

\begin{equation*}
    \sigma_{es} = a_s + b_s T_e + c_s T_e^2 \hspace{5mm} \text{Ref. \cite{gnoffo1989}}
\end{equation*}

The constants for curve fits for the neutral species is found in Table [\ref{tab:el-neut-x-sec}].

\begin{table}[ht]
\centering
\begin{tabular}{cccc}
\hline
$s$ & $a_s$ & $b_s$ & $c_s$ \\
\hline
$N_2$ & $7.5\times10^{-20}$ & $5.5\times10^{-24}$ & $-1.0\times10^{-28}$ \\
$O_2$ & $2.0\times10^{-20}$ & $6.0\times10^{-24}$ & $0.0$ \\
$NO$ & $1.0\times10^{-19}$ & $0.0$ & $0.0$ \\
$N$ & $5.0\times10^{-20}$ & $0.0$ & $0.0$ \\
$O$ & $1.2\times10^{-20}$ & $1.7\times10^{-24}$ & $-2.0\times10^{-29}$ \\
\hline
\end{tabular}
\caption{Curve fit constants for electron-neutral energy exchange cross sections $\sigma_{es}$. Ref. \cite{gnoffo1989}}
\label{tab:el-neut-x-sec}
\end{table}

\subsection{Vibrational-Electron Energy Relaxation}
\label{sec:vib-el-relax}

Energy exchange between free electrons and vibrational modes is either neglected, or only modelled for Nitrogen, as there is a strong resonance seen in Nitrogen's electronic ground state $X^1 \Sigma_g$. This reaction is described as:

\begin{equation*}
    e^- + N_2(X^1 \Sigma_g^+;v) \rightarrow N_2^-(^2 \Pi_g) \rightarrow e^- + N_2(X^1 \Sigma_g^+; w)
\end{equation*}

Here, a free electron collides with an $N_2$ molecule and briefly attaches and then detaches. However, this inelastic collision increases the vibrational level of $N_2$ from $v$ to $w$. A number of curve fits exist to compute this energy source term where a Landau-Teller form is used:

\begin{equation*}
    \mathcal{S}_{v-e}^{ve} = \frac{e_v(T_e) - e_v(T_v)}{\tau_{ve}}
\end{equation*}

One earlier model for the relaxation time is proposed by Ref. \cite{candler1988}:

\begin{equation}
\log\!\left(p_e \tau_e\right)=
\begin{cases}
7.50(\log T_e)^2 - 57.0\log T_e + 98.70,
& T_e < 7000~\si{\kelvin}, \\[0.5em]
2.36(\log T_e)^2 - 17.9\log T_e + 24.35,
& T_e > 7000~\si{\kelvin}.
\end{cases}
\end{equation}

Where $\log$ is in base 10, and the electron pressure is in atmospheres. A more recent model from Ref. \cite{laporta2013} proposes the following curve fit:

\begin{equation*}
    p_e \tau_e = p_e \tau_e^\text{min} + \sum_{i=1}^3 c_i (T_e - T_e^\text{min})^i, \hspace{5mm} T_e \geq T_e^\text{min}
\end{equation*}

With the following curve fit parameters:

\begin{align*}
    c_1 &= 2.38 \times 10^{-5} - 4.54 \times 10^{-9} T_v + 1.65 \times 10^{-13} T_v^2 \\
    c_2 &= 1.78 \times 10^{-8} + 2.11 \times 10^{-12} T_v - 2.23 \times 10^{-17} T_v^2 \\
    c_3 &= 1.17 \times 10^{-13} \\
\end{align*}

And:

\begin{align*}
    T_e^\text{min} = \frac{b}{T_v + a}, &\hspace{5mm} p_e \tau_e^ \text{min} = c + d T_v \\
    a = 2.21599e4 \si{\kelvin}, &\hspace{5mm} b = 1.60e8 \si{\kelvin\squared} \\
    c = 1.20e-5\si{\joule\per\meter\tothe{3}}, &\hspace{5mm} d = 5.90e-10 \si{\joule\per\meter\tothe{3}\second\per\kelvin}
\end{align*}

If modelling of energy transfer between $O_2$ and free electrons is desired, more curve fits have been generated. Ref \cite{laporta2016} gives the following curve fit equation for the interval 2000 K $\leq T_e \leq$ 20,000 K, and 0 $\leq T_v \leq$ 20.000 K:

\begin{equation}
    p_e \tau_e = \sum_{n=0}^4 \sum_{m=0}^2 c_{n,m} T_e^n T_v^m
\end{equation}

Where the constants $c_{n,m}$ are given in Table [\ref{tab:o2-electron-constants}].

\begin{table}[h!]
\centering
\begin{tabular}{cccc}
\hline
n/m & 0 & 1 & 2 \\
\hline
0 & $2.418 \times 10^0$ & $-2.660 \times 10^{-4}$ & $1.015 \times 10^{-8}$ \\
1 & $-2.094 \times 10^{-3}$ & $3.537 \times 10^{-7}$ & $-1.153 \times 10^{-11}$ \\
2 & $7.174 \times 10^{-7}$ & $-7.578 \times 10^{-11}$ & $2.442 \times 10^{-15}$ \\
3 &$ -4.511 \times 10^{-11}$ & $4.435 \times 10^{-15}$ & $-1.407 \times 10^{-19}$ \\
4 & $8.682 \times 10^{-16}$ & $-8.131 \times 10^{-20}$ & $2.610 \times 10^{-24}$ \\
\hline
\end{tabular}
\caption{Curve fit constants $c_{n,m}$ for $O_2$ vibrational-electron energy exchange. Ref \cite{laporta2016}.}
\label{tab:o2-electron-constants}
\end{table}


\subsection{Electron Impact Ionization Source Term}
\label{sec:el_ion_source}
Energy loss due to electron impact ionization is a source term that needs to be modelled in a two-temperature vibrational-electronic-electron or three-temperature electronic-electron conservation equation. The term is denoted as:

\begin{equation*}
    \mathcal{S}_{e-i}^{ve} = \sum_{s}^\text{NI} \dot{n}_{e,s} \hat{I}_s
\end{equation*}

Where the sum is taken over all ionized species undergoing an electron impact ionization reaction. $\dot{n}_{e,s}$ is the molar rate of ionization. In Park's models, there are only two electron impact ionization reactions:

\begin{align*}
    N + e^- \leftrightarrow N^+ + e^- + e^- \\
    O + e^- \leftrightarrow O^+ + e^- + e^-
\end{align*}

Here, $\dot{n}_{e,s}$ is taken as the rate of the forward reaction. The first ionization energies for neutral species is shown in Table [\ref{tab:first-ion-energy}].

\begin{table}[ht]
\centering
\begin{tabular}{cccc}
\hline
$s$ & $\hat{I}_s$ (eV) & $\hat{I}_s$ (\si{\joule\per\kilo\mole}) & $\hat{I}_s$ (\si{\joule\per\kilo\gram}) \\
\hline
$N_2$ & 15.51 & $1.4967 \times 10^9$ & $5.343 \times 10^7$ \\
$O_2$ & 12.5 & $1.2063 \times 10^9$ & $3.770 \times 10^7$ \\
$NO$ & 9.5 & $0.9167 \times 10^9$ & $3.055 \times 10^7$ \\
$N$ & 14.53 & $1.4021 \times 10^9$ & $1.0010 \times 10^8$ \\
$O$ & 13.614 & $1.3138 \times 10^9$ & $8.211 \times 10^7$ \\
\hline
\end{tabular}
\caption{Valus for the first ionization energies $\hat{I}_s$ of neutral species in different units. Ref. \cite{gnoffo1989}}
\label{tab:first-ion-energy}
\end{table}


\subsection{Vibration-Chemical Energy Relaxation}
\label{sec:vib-chem-source}

The vibrational energy lost or gained due to depletion of vibrationally excited molecules through dissociation or recombination is a source term that needs to be modelled in the vibrational energy conservation equation. It is expressed by:

\begin{equation}
    \mathcal{S}_{v-c}^{v} = \sum_{s}^\text{ND} \mathcal{S}_{v-c,s}^{v} = \sum_s^\text{ND} \dot{w}_s \hat{D}_s
\end{equation}

Where $\dot{\omega}_s$ is the rate at which species $s$ is created or destroyed, and $\hat{D}_s$ is representative of the amount of vibrational energy that is associated with that molecule. A non-preferential method assumes that the vibrational energy associated with the molecule can be taken as the average among molecules of species $s$, i.e. $\hat{D}_s = e_{v,s}(T_v)$. This is an incomplete description, however, since it is believed that molecules at an elevated vibrational state are more likely to dissociate, and hence the non-preferential method tends to underestimate the amount of vibrational energy destroyed in a dissociation reaction.

\hspace{5mm}
Other appraoches assume is that the value of $\hat{D}_s$ is taken as the product of a constant and the dissociation energy of the molecule, $\tilde{D}_s$, or a constant and the average vibrational energy of species $s$:

\begin{align*}
    \hat{D}_s &= c_1 \tilde{D}_s \\
    \hat{D}_s &= c_2 e_{v,s}(T_v)
\end{align*}

In these cases, $c_1$ is a value less that 1.0, with some examples being 0.8, or 0.3. $c_2$ is a constant higher than 1.0, to model that the vibrational energy dissociate is indeed higher than $e_{v,s}(T_v)$. The dissociation energies for air molecules $\tilde{D}_s$ are shown in Table [\ref{tab:diss-energies}]

\begin{table}[ht]
\centering
\begin{tabular}{cccc}
\hline
$s$ & $\tilde{D}_s$ (eV) & $\tilde{D}_s$ (\si{\joule\per\kilo\mole}) & $\tilde{D}_s$ (\si{\joule\per\kilo\gram}) \\
\hline
$N_2$ & 9.759 & $9.417 \times 10^8$ & $3.3618 \times 10^7$ \\
$O_2$ & 5.115 & $4.936 \times 10^8$ & $1.5426 \times 10^7$ \\
$NO$ & 6.496 & $6.269 \times 10^8$ & $2.0891 \times 10^7$ \\
$N_2^+$ & 8.712 & $8.407 \times 10^8$ & $3.0011 \times 10^7$ \\
$O_2^+$ & 6.663 & $6.430 \times 10^8$ & $2.0097 \times 10^7$ \\
$NO^+$ & 10.85 & $1.047 \times 10^9$ & $3.4894 \times 10^7$ \\
\hline
\end{tabular}
\caption{Values for the dissociation energies $\tilde{D}_s$ of molecules in different units. Ref. \cite{gnoffo1989}}
\label{tab:diss-energies}
\end{table}

\newpage
\section{Nonequilibrium Conservation Equations}

In the following sections, we show the governing equations for a chemically reacting flow in thermal nonequilibrium. In each section, we show a conservation equation with alternate forms as well as the physical meaning of each term and explanations of variables. Included at the end of each equation is the reference the equation was taken from.

\subsection{Species Mass Conservation}

\begin{equation}
\underbracket{ \pd{}{t} \rho_s}_1 + \underbracket{\pd{}{x_j} \rho_s u_j}_2 = \underbracket{\pd{}{x_j} (\rho D_s \pd{}{x_j} y_s)}_3 + \underbracket{\dot{\omega}_s}_4  \hspace{5mm}  \text{Ref. \cite{gnoffo1989}}
\end{equation}

\begin{equation} \pd{}{t} \rho_s + \nabla \cdot (\rho_s \textbf{u}) + \nabla \cdot (\rho_s \boldsymbol{V}_s) = \dot{\omega}_s \hspace{5mm} \text{Ref. \cite{scogginsthesis}}
\end{equation}

The terms are:

\begin{enumerate}
    \item Time rate of change of $s$-species mass.
    \item Flux of mass due to convection.
    \item Molecular diffusion flux.
    \item Chemical production rate due to reactions. This term is explained in Sec. [\ref{sec:chem_source}].
\end{enumerate}

The variables are:
\begin{itemize}
    \item $\rho_s$ is the density of species $s$.
    \item $u_j$ is the mass-averaged velocity in the $j$-direction.
    \item $D_s$ is the effective diffusion coefficient of species $s$.
    \item $y_s$ is the molar fraction of species $s$.
    \item $\dot{\omega}_s$ is the chemical production rate of species $s$.
    \item $\boldsymbol{V}_s$ is the diffusion velocity of species $s$.
\end{itemize}

\begin{definition}[Molecular diffusion flux]
There are a number of ways to express the molecular diffusion flux. Sometimes it is given as $J_s$ = $\rho_s \boldsymbol{V}_s$. From the above equation, we can see that $J_s$ is also equivalent to $\rho D_s \nabla y_s$. There are many different methods to compute the diffusion flux. Some examples are Fick's law, the modified Fick's law, and the Stefan-Maxwell equations. These methods are discussed in more detail in the next section. It is important to note that the sum of the diffusion fluxes across all species is zero, as this enforces mass conservation:
\end{definition}


\subsection{Total Continuity}
For chemically reacting flows, the total continuity equation is unaffected. It is:


\begin{equation*}
    \pd{\rho}{t} + \pd{}{x_j} (\rho u_j) = 0
\end{equation*}

\begin{equation}
    \pd{\rho}{t} + \nabla \cdot (\rho \textbf{u}) = 0
\end{equation}

Since no mass can be created or destroyed, the condition of atomic conservation is applied, which yields:

\begin{equation}
    \sum_{s} \dot{w}_s = 0
\end{equation}

\subsection{Mixture Momentum Conservation}

\begin{equation}
    \underbracket{\pd{}{t} \rho u_i}_{1} + \underbracket{\pd{}{x_j} \rho u_i u_j}_{2} = \underbracket{-\pd{p}{x_i}}_{3} + \underbracket{\pd{}{x_j} \left[ \mu \left( \pd{u_i}{x_j} + \pd{u_j}{x_i} \right) - \frac{2}{3} \mu \pd{u_k}{x_k} \delta_{ij} \right]}_4 \hspace{5mm} \text{Ref. \cite{gnoffo1989}}
\end{equation}

\begin{equation*}
    \pd{}{t} \rho \boldsymbol{u} + \nabla \cdot (\rho \textbf{u} \otimes \textbf{u}) = -\nabla p + \nabla \cdot \boldsymbol{\bar{\Pi}} + \ub{nq\boldsymbol{E}' + \boldsymbol{j} \times \boldsymbol{B}}{5} \hspace{5mm} \text{Ref. \cite{scogginsthesis}}
\end{equation*}

\begin{equation*}
    \pd{}{t} (\rho u_i) + \pd{}{x_j} (\rho u_i u_j + p \delta_{ij}) = - \pd{\tau_{ij}}{x_j} - \sum_{i=1}^\text{NS} \frac{N_s}{N_e} Z_s \pd{p_e}{x_i} \hspace{5mm} \text{Ref. \cite{candlerthesis}}
\end{equation*}

The terms are:

\begin{enumerate}
    \item Time rate of change of $i$th component of momentum.
    \item Flux of $i$th component of momentum due to convection.
    \item Pressure forces in $i$th direction.
    \item Viscous terms acting in $i$th direction.
    \item Electric field forces acting on the mixture.
\end{enumerate}

The variables are:

\begin{itemize}
    \item $\rho u_i$ is the mass averaged momentum in the $i$ direction.
    \item $u_j$ is the mass averaged velocity in the $j$ direction.
    \item $p$ is the pressure of the mixture compute with Dalton's law of partial pressures.
    \item $\mu$ is the mixture viscosity.
    \item $n$ is the mixture number density.
    \item $q = \sum_s X_s q_s$ is the mixture charge.
    \item $\boldsymbol{E}' = \boldsymbol{E} + \boldsymbol{u} \times \boldsymbol{B}$ is the electric field in the hydrodynamic velocity frame. Here, $\boldsymbol{E}$ is the electric field while $\boldsymbol{B}$ is the magnetic field.
    \item $\boldsymbol{j} = \sum_s \boldsymbol{j}_s = \sum_s n_s q_s \boldsymbol{V}_s$ is the mixture conduction current.
\end{itemize}

\subsection{Total Rotational Energy Conservation}

\begin{equation*}
    \ub{\pd{}{t} (\rho e_r)}{1} + \ub{\pd{}{x_j} (\rho e_r u_j)}{2} = \ub{\pd{}{x_j} \bp{\kappa_r \pd{T_r}{x_j}}}{3} + \ub{\mathcal{S}_{t-r}^{r}}{4} + \ub{\mathcal{S}_{e-r}^{re}}{5} + \ub{\mathcal{S}_{r-c}^{r}}{6} \hspace{5mm} \text{Ref. \cite{gnoffo1989}}
\end{equation*}

\subsection{Total Vibrational Energy Conservation}

\begin{multline}
    \ub{\pd{}{t} \rho e_v}{1} + \ub{\pd{}{x_j}\rho e_v u_j}{2} = \ub{\pd{}{x_j} \bp{\kappa_v \pd{T_v}{x_j}}}{3} \\ + \ub{\pd{}{x_j} \bp{\rho \sum_{s=1}^\text{NS} h_{v,s} D_s \pd{y_s}{x_j}}}{4} + \ub{\mathcal{S}_{t-v}^{v}}{5} + \ub{\mathcal{S}_{e-v}^{ve}}{6} + \ub{\mathcal{S}_{v-c}^{v}}{7} \hspace{5mm} \text{Ref. \cite{gnoffo1989}}
    \label{eq:cons_vib_energy}
\end{multline}

The terms are:

\begin{enumerate}
    \item Rate of change of vibrational energy per unit volume.
    \item The flux of vibrational energy due to convection.
    \item The conduction of vibrational energy due to vibrational temperature gradients.
    \item The diffusion of vibrational energy due to molecular concentration gradients.
    \item The energy exchange (relaxation) between vibrational and translational modes due to collisions within the cell. It is denoted as $\mathcal{S}_{t-v}^v$ and explained in Sec. [\ref{sec:vib-trans-relax}]
    \item The energy exchange between vibrational and electronic modes. It is explained in Sec. [\ref{sec:el-trans-relax}].
    \item The vibrational lost/gained due to molecular depletion (dissociation) or production (recombination). It is explained in [\ref{sec:vib-chem-source}].
\end{enumerate}

The variables are:

\begin{itemize}
    \item $e_v$ is the vibrational energy per unit mass, defined as $\displaystyle e_v = \sum_{s=1}^\text{NS} \frac{\rho_s e_{v,s}}{\rho}$. $e_v$ is defined as a function of $T_v$, the vibrational temperature.
    \item $\kappa_v$ is the vibrational thermal conductivity.
    \item $h_{v,s}$ is the vibrational enthalpy. It is identical to $e_{v,s}$.
    \item $e_{v,s}^*$ is the vibrational energy of species $s$ evaluated at the translational-rotational temperature.
    \item $e_{v,s}^{**}$ is the vibrational energy evaluated at the electron temperature. 
    \item $<\tau_s>$ is the characteristic relaxation times for translational-vibrational (T-V) energy exchange.
    \item $<\tau_{es}>$ is the characteristic relation times for electron-vibrational (e-V) energy exchange.
    \item $\hat{D}_s$ is the vibration energy level representative of those molecules of species $s$ which are created or destroyed (recombined or dissociated) because of their high vibrational quantum numbers.
\end{itemize}

\subsection{Species Vibrational Energy Conservation}

\begin{multline}
    \ub{\pd{E_{v,s}}{t}}{1} + \ub{\pd{}{x_j} (E_{v,s} u_j)}{2} = - \ub{\pd{}{x_j} (E_{v,s} v_{s,j})}{3} - \ub{\pd{q_{v,s,j}}{x_j}}{4} + \ub{\mathcal{S}_{t-v,s}^{v}}{5} + \ub{\mathcal{S}_{v-v,s}^{v}}{6} + \ub{\mathcal{S}_{e-v,s}^{ev}}{7} + \ub{\mathcal{S}_{v-c,s}^{v}}{8} \hspace{5mm} \text{Ref. \cite{candlerthesis}}
\end{multline}

The terms are:

\begin{enumerate}
    \item The time rate of change of the vibrational energy of species $s$.
    \item The flux of vibrational energy of species $s$ due to convection.
    \item The flux of vibrational energy due to mass diffusion of species $s$.
    \item The flux of vibrational energy due to conduction.
    \item The energy exchange between translational modes of all species and the vibrational mode of species $s$. This term is explained in Sec. [\ref{sec:vib-trans-relax}].
    \item The energy exchange between the vibrational modes of other species and the vibrational mode of species $s$. This term is explained in Sec. [\ref{sec:vib-vib-relax}].
    \item The energy exchange between the free electrons and the vibrational mode of species $s$. This term is explained in Sec. [\ref{sec:vib-el-relax}].
    \item The energy lost or gained due to the depletion or production of species $s$ through chemical reactions. This term is explained in Sec. [\ref{sec:vib-chem-source}].
\end{enumerate}


\subsection{Electron-Electronic Energy Conservation}

\begin{multline}
    \ub{\pd{}{t} \rho e_e}{1} + \ub{\pd{}{x_j} \bb{u_j(\rho e_e + p_e)}}{2} = \ub{u_j \pd{p_e}{x_j} }{3} + \ub{\pd{}{x_j} \bp{\kappa_e \pd{T_e}{x_j}}}{4} + \ub{\pd{}{x_j} \bp{\rho \sum_{s=1}^\text{NS} h_{e,s} D_s \pd{y_s}{x_j}}}{5} \\ + \ub{\mathcal{S}_{t-e}^e}{6}  - \ub{\mathcal{S}_{e-i}^{e}}{7} - \ub{\mathcal{S}_{v-e}^{ve}}{8} - \ub{Q_\text{rad}}{9} \hspace{5mm} \text{Ref. \cite{gnoffo1989}}
\end{multline}

Terms are:

\begin{enumerate}
    \item Rate of change of electronic energy per unit volume.
    \item The flux of electronic enthalpy due to convection.
    \item The work done on electrons by an electric field incudes by an electron pressure gradient.
    \item The conduction of electronic energy due to electron temperature gradient.
    \item The diffuction of electronic energy due to concentration gradients.
    \item The energy exchange due to elastic collisions between electrons and heavy particles. Its expression is explained in Sec. [\ref{sec:el-trans-relax}].
    \item Energy loss due to electron impact ionization. It is expression is explained in Sec. [\ref{sec:el_ion_source}].
    \item The energy lost due to inelastic collisions between electrons and molecules, where electron energy is transfered into vibrational energy states of the molecule. It is the same term that appears in in the vibrational energy equation, but note that the sign is reversed. It's expression is detailed in Sec. [\ref{sec:vib-el-relax}]
    \item Rate of energy loss due to radiation caused by electronic transitions.
\end{enumerate}

The variables are:
\begin{itemize}
    \item $e_e$ is the electronic energy per unit mass and contains contributions from electronic energy levels. It is defined as $\displaystyle e_e = \sum_{s=1}^\text{NS} \frac{\rho_s e_{e,s}}{\rho}$
    \item  $e_{e,s}$ is the electronic energy per unit mass of species $s$ and is defined as a function of $T_e$.
    \item $T_e$ is the electron temperature.
    \item $\eta_e$ is the is the electronic thermal conductivity.
    \item $h_{e,s}$ is the electronic enthalpy per unit mass and is idential to $e_{e,s}$ for all species except free electrons. For free electrons it is defined as: $ \displaystyle h_{e,e} = e_{e,e} + \frac{\hat{R}T_e}{\mathcal{M}_e}$
    \item $\nu_{e,s}$ is the effective collision frequency of electrons with heavy particles.
    \item $\dot{n}_{e,s}$ is the molar rate of production of species $s$ by electron impact ionizations.
    \item $\hat{I}_s$ is the energy per unit mole lost by a free electron in producing species $s$ through electron impact ionization.
    \item $Q_\text{rad}$ is the radiant energy transfer rate due to electron transitions.
\end{itemize}

\subsection{Electron Energy Conservation}

In some methods, electronic energy is included in the total energy and evaluated at the electron temperature, but the free-electron energy is separated as its own conservation equation. Therefore, the electron temperature is evolved only because of the electron energy equation, and the electronic energy is assumed to be in equilibrium with the electron temperature. The electron energy conservation equation is:

\begin{equation}
\ub{\pd{E_e}{t}}{1} + \ub{\pd{}{x_j} \bp{E_e u_j}}{2} = - \ub{\pd{}{x_j} \bp{E_e v_{ej}}}{3} - \ub{p_e \pd{u_j}{x_j}}{4} - \ub{\pd{q_{ej}}{x_j}}{5} + \ub{\mathcal{S}_{t-e}^e}{6} - \ub{\mathcal{S}_{e-v}^{e}}{7} + \ub{\mathcal{S}_{e-c}^{e}}{8} \hspace{5mm} \text{Ref. \cite{candlerthesis}}
\end{equation}

Here, $E_e$ is defined as the combinationation of the thermal and kinetic energy of the free electrons:

\begin{equation*}
    E_e = \rho_e c_{ve} T_e + \frac{1}{2} \rho_e (u^2 + v^2 + w^2)
\end{equation*}

The terms are:

\begin{enumerate}
    \item The time rate of change of the electron energy.
    \item The flux of electron energy due to convection.
    \item The flux of electron energy due to molecular diffusion.
    \item The work done on electrons by an electric field incudes by an electron pressure gradient.
    \item The flux of electron energy due to conduction.
    \item The energy exchange between translational modes of all species and the electronic mode of species $s$. This term is explained in Sec. [\ref{sec:el-trans-relax}].
    \item The energy exchange between the vibrational modes of other species and the electronic mode of species $s$. This term is explained in Sec. [\ref{sec:vib-el-relax}].
    \item The energy lost or gained due to the depletion or production of species $s$   
\end{enumerate}


\subsection{Total Energy Conservation}

\begin{multline}
    \ub{\pd{}{t} \rho E}{1} + \ub{\pd{}{x_j} \rho H u_j}{2} = \ub{\pd{}{x_j} \bp{\eta \pd{T}{x_j} + \eta_v \pd{T_v}{x_j} + \eta_e \pd{T_e}{x_j}}}{3} \\ + \ub{\pd{}{x_j} \bp{\rho \sum_{s=1}^\text{NS} h_s D_s \pd{y_s}{x_j}}}{4} + \ub{\pd{}{x_j} \bb{u_i \mu \bp{\pd{u_i}{x_j} + \pd{u_j}{x_i}}}}{5} - \ub{Q_\text{rad}}{6}
\end{multline}

The terms are:

\begin{enumerate}
    \item Rate of change of total energy per unit volume.
    \item The flux of total enthalpy due to convection.
    \item The conduction of energy due to temperature, vibrational temperature, and electron temperature gradients.
    \item The diffusion of energy due to concentration gradients.
    \item The word done by shear forces.
    \item The rate of energy loss due to radiation caused by electronic transitions.
\end{enumerate}

The variables are:

\begin{itemize}
    \item $E$ is the total energy per unit volume, defined as $\displaystyle E = \frac{u_i u_i}{2} + \sum_{s=1}^\text{NS} \frac{\rho_s e_s}{\rho}$
    \item $H$ is the total enthalpy per unit mass, defined as $\displaystyle H = E + \frac{p}{\rho}$
    \item $\eta$ is the frozen thermal conductivity of heavy particles. The part in which exchanges of translational and rotational energy occur.
\end{itemize}

\subsection{Two temperature model}

Everything before this assumes a three-temperature model, where there is a single temperature dedicated to translational-rotational energy, vibrational energy, and electronic-electron energies. The two temperature model assumes that one temperature can be used for describing the energy of translational-rotational energies, and then the rest (vib-electronic-electron) are desibed by the second temperature, $T_V$. Therefore:

\begin{equation}
    T_v = T_e = T_{ve}
\end{equation}

By combining the vibrational and electronic energy equations, we get the vibrational-electronic energy conservation equation:

\begin{multline}
    \ub{ \pd{}{t} \rho e_{ve}}{1} + \ub{\pd{}{x_j} \rho e_{ve} u_j}{2} = \ub{- p_e \pd{u_j}{x_j}}{3} + \ub{\pd{}{x_j} \bb{(\eta_v + \eta_e) \pd{T_{ve}}{x_j}}}{4} + \ub{\pd{}{x_j} \bp{\rho \sum_{s=1}^\text{NS} h_{{ve},s} D_s \pd{y_s}{x_j}}}{5} \\ + \ub{\mathcal{S}_{t-v}^v}{6} + \ub{\mathcal{S}_{t-e}^{ve}}{7}  - \ub{\mathcal{S}_{e-i}^{ve}}{8} + \ub{\mathcal{S}_{v-c}^{v}}{9}- \ub{Q_\text{rad}}{10}
\end{multline}

\newpage
\section{Partial Derivatives for Noequilibrium Jacobians}

In this section, partial derivatives are given for numerous flow quanties for use in assembling the Jacobian matrices for the flow solver.

\subsubsection{Partial derivatives of pressure ($p$)}

\begin{subequations}
    \begin{align}
        \pd{p}{\rho_s} &= \bp{\frac{\hat{R}}{\mathcal{M}_s} - c_{vs} \frac{R}{c_v}} T + \frac{R}{c_v} \bb{\frac{1}{2} (u^2 + v^2 + w^2) - h_s^\circ}
    \\ \pd{p}{(\rho u)} &= -u \frac{R}{c_v}
    \\ \pd{p}{(\rho v)} &= -v \frac{R}{c_v}
    \\ \pd{p}{(\rho w)} &= -w \frac{R}{c_v}
    \\ \pd{p}{E_v} &= -\frac{R}{c_v}
    \\ \pd{p}{E} &= \frac{R}{c_v}
    \end{align}
\end{subequations}

\subsubsection{Partial derivatives of total energy ($E$)}

\begin{subequations}
    \begin{align}
        \pd{E}{\rho_s} &= \bp{c_{vs} - \frac{c_v}{R} \frac{\hat{R}}{\mathcal{M}_s}}T + \frac{1}{2} (u^2 + v^2 + w^2) + e_v + h_s^\circ
        \\ \pd{E}{u} &= \rho u 
        \\ \pd{E}{v} &= \rho v
        \\ \pd{E}{w} &= \rho w
        \\ \pd{E}{e_v} &= \sum_{s=1}^\text{NS} \rho_s
        \\ \pd{E}{p} &= \frac{c_v}{R}
    \end{align}
\end{subequations}


\subsubsection{Partial derivatives of $T$}

Note that for a general $n$-temperature model, the translational temperature is defined as:

\begin{equation*}
    T_{tr}(\boldsymbol{U}) = \frac{1}{\rho c_v^*} \bb{\rho E - \text{KE} - \sum_s^\mathcal{S} \rho_s h_s^\circ - \sum_s^\mathcal{P} \rho_s e_{v,s} (T_v) - \sum_s^\mathcal{H} \rho_s e_{e,s} (T_e) - \rho_e e_{tr, e} (T_e)}
\end{equation*}

Here, $\text{KE} = \frac{1}{2} \rho (u^2 + v^2 + w^2)$ is the kinetic energy per unit volume, $\mathcal{P}$ is the set of polyatomic molecules that contain vibrational energy, and $\mathcal{H}$ is the set of heavy species that contain electronic energy. For a one-temperature model, you can set $T_v = T_e = T$ and the vibrational and electronic energy terms will be evaluated at the translational temperature, or neglected if these energy modes are to be ignored. If  

\begin{subequations}
    \begin{align}
        \pd{T}{\rho_s} &= \frac{1}{\rho c_v^*} \bb{-c_{v,s}T + \frac{1}{2} (u^2 + v^2 + w^2) - h_s^\circ}
        \\ \textcolor{blue}{\pd{T}{\rho_e}} &\textcolor{blue}{= \frac{1}{\rho c_v^*} \bb{\frac{1}{2} (u^2 + v^2 + w^2) - h_{f,e}^\circ} \hspace{5mm} \text{(for three temperature models)}}
        \\ \pd{T}{(\rho u)} &= -\frac{u}{\rho c_v^*}
        \\ \pd{T}{(\rho v)} &= -\frac{v}{\rho c_v^*}
        \\ \pd{T}{(\rho w)} &= -\frac{w}{\rho c_v^*}
        \\ \pd{T}{E} &= \frac{1}{\rho c_v^*}
        \\ \textcolor{red}{\pd{T}{E_v}} &\textcolor{red}{= -\frac{1}{\rho c_v^*}}
        \\ \textcolor{red}{\pd{T}{E_{v,s}}} &\textcolor{red}{= -\frac{1}{\rho c_v^*}}
        \\ \textcolor{blue}{\pd{T}{E_e}} &\textcolor{blue}{= -\frac{1}{\rho c_v^*}}
    \end{align}
\end{subequations}

\subsubsection{Partial derivatives of vibrational temperature ($T_v$)}


For one vibrational temperature equation, the derivative includes sums over all vibrational species. When a separate vibrational temperature is solved for each species, the form remains the same but the sums are not present:

\begin{multicols}{2}
    
\begin{subequations}
    \begin{align}
    \pd{T_v}{\rho_i} &= -e_{vi} / \sum_{s=1}^\text{NS} \bb{\rho_s \bp{\pd{e_{vs}}{T_v}}}
    \\ \pd{T_v}{(\rho u)} &= 0
    \\ \pd{T_v}{(\rho v)} &= 0
    \\ \pd{T_v}{(\rho w)} &= 0
    \\ \pd{T_v}{E} &= 0
    \\ \pd{T_v}{E_v} &= 1 / \sum_{s=1}^\text{NS} \bb{\rho_s \bp{\pd{e_{vs}}{T_v}}}
    \\ \pd{T_v}{E_e} &= 0
    \end{align}
\end{subequations}

\begin{subequations}
    \begin{align}
    \pd{T_{v,s}}{\rho_s} &= -e_{v,s} / \bp{\rho_s \pd{e_{v,s}}{T_{v,s}}}
    \\ \pd{T_{v,s}}{(\rho u)} &= 0
    \\ \pd{T_{v,s}}{(\rho v)} &= 0
    \\ \pd{T_{v,s}}{(\rho w)} &= 0
    \\ \pd{T_{v,s}}{E} &= 0
    \\ \pd{T_{v,s}}{E_{v,s}} &= 1 / \bp{\rho_s \pd{e_{v,s}}{T_{v,s}}}
    \\ \pd{T_{v,s}}{E_e} &= 0
    \end{align}
\end{subequations}

\end{multicols}

\subsubsection{Partial derivatives of vibrational energy ($e_vs$)}

\begin{subequations}
    \begin{align}
        \pd{e_{vs}}{\rho_i} = \bp{\pd{e_{vs}}{T_v}}_{T_v} \pd{T_v}{\rho_i}, &\hspace{10mm} \pd{e_{vs}^*}{\rho_i} =  \bp{\pd{e_{vs}}{T_v}}_{T_v  = T} \pd{T_v}{\rho_i}
        \\ \pd{e_{vs}}{(\rho u)} = \bp{\pd{e_{vs}}{T_v}}_{T_v} \pd{T_v}{(\rho u)}, &\hspace{10mm} \pd{e_{vs}^*}{(\rho u)} = \bp{\pd{e_{vs}}{T_v}}_{T_v  = T} \pd{T_v}{(\rho u)}
        \\ \pd{e_{vs}}{(\rho v)} = \bp{\pd{e_{vs}}{T_v}}_{T_v} \pd{T_v}{(\rho v)}, &\hspace{10mm} \pd{e_{vs}^*}{(\rho v)} = \bp{\pd{e_{vs}}{T_v}}_{T_v  = T} \pd{T_v}{(\rho v)}
        \\ \pd{e_{vs}}{(\rho w)} = \bp{\pd{e_{vs}}{T_v}}_{T_v} \pd{T_v}{(\rho w)}, &\hspace{10mm} \pd{e_{vs}^*}{(\rho w)} = \bp{\pd{e_{vs}}{T_v}}_{T_v  = T} \pd{T_v}{(\rho w)}
        \\ \pd{e_{vs}}{E_v} = \bp{\pd{e_{vs}}{T_v}}_{T_v} \pd{T_v}{E_v}, &\hspace{10mm} \pd{e_{vs}^*}{E_v} = \bp{\pd{e_{vs}}{T_v}}_{T_v  = T} \pd{T_v}{E_v}
        \\ \pd{e_{vs}}{E} = \bp{\pd{e_{vs}}{T_v}}_{T_v} \pd{T_v}{E}, &\hspace{10mm} \pd{e_{vs}^*}{E} = \bp{\pd{e_{vs}}{T_v}}_{T_v  = T} \pd{T_v}{E}
    \end{align}    
\end{subequations}




\subsubsection{Explicit 0D Reactor}

With no fluxes, the updated equation for the 0D reactor is:

\begin{equation}
    \frac{U^{n+1} - U^n}{\Delta t} = S(U^n)
\end{equation}

or:

\begin{equation}
    \delta U = U^{n+1} - U^n, \hspace{5mm} \delta U = \Delta t S(U^n)
\end{equation}

\subsubsection{Implicit 0D Reactor}

The backward Euler time discretization for the 0D reactor look like:

\begin{equation}
    \frac{U^{n+1} - U^n}{\Delta t} = S(U^{n+1})
\end{equation}

Linearize the source term as:

\begin{equation}
    S(U^{n+1}) = S(U^n) + \pd{S(U^n)}{U_j} \delta U_j
\end{equation}

And the final equation is:

\begin{equation}
    \bb{I - \Delta t J} \delta U = \Delta t S
\end{equation}

Then:

\begin{equation}
    U^{n+1} = U^n + \delta U
\end{equation}

The nonlinear residual is:

\begin{equation}
    R(U) = U - U^n - \Delta t S(U)
\end{equation}

Where $U$ = $U^{n+1}$ or $U^{k+1}$. We aim to find a $U$ that satisfies $R(U^{n+1})$ = 0. Therefore, we use Newton steps to find this value. First, take your current $U_n$, and set it as $U_k$. 

\newpage
\section{Stagnation Line Solver for Nonequilibirum Flow with Arbitrary Number of Energy Equations}

The Dimensionally-Reduced Navier-Stokes Equations (DRNSE) are found by first casting the Navier-Stokes equations into cylindrical or polar coordinates and then using a separation of variables technique to cast certain flow quantities as functions of the radial coordinate $r$ only. An example is shown in the next section by introducing the conservation of electronic-electron internal energy equation into the DRNSE.

\subsection{Derivation of Electron Energy Conservation Equation for the DRNSE}

First, we show the electronic-electron energy in cartesian coordinates again, grouping source terms together into $S_e$ for simplicity. The "$el$" subscript denotes an electronic equantiy, while "$e$" denotes an electron quantity.

\begin{equation*}
    \pd{}{t} \rho e_{e} + \pd{}{x_j} \bb{u_j(\rho e_{el-e} + p_e)} + \pd{q_j^{el-e}}{x_j} = u_j \pd{p_e}{x_j} + S_{el-e} \hspace{5mm}
\end{equation*}

We use the following relationships from Ref. \cite{klomfass1996} to cast the divergences of scalars, vectors, and tensors in cartesian coordinates into polar coordinates, throwing away any derivatives with respect to the aximuthal angle, $\phi$, due to the assumption of rotationally-symmetric flow:

\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
\pd{}{x_j} \cdot (p \boldsymbol{I}) &= \pd{p}{r} + \frac{1}{r} \pd{p}{\theta} \\
\pd{}{x_j} \cdot (f(r) u_j) &= \pd{}{r}[f(r) u_r] + \frac{2}{r} f(r) u_r + \frac{\cot \theta}{r} f(r) u_\theta + \frac{1}{r} \pd{}{\theta} [f(r) u_\theta]
\label{eq:polar_div}
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}

The separation of variables technique assumes that we can cast a flow quantity dependant on $r$ and $\theta$ as the product of a function of $r$ and a function of $\theta$:

\begin{equation*}
    F(r, \theta) = \bar{F}(r) F^*(\theta)
\end{equation*}

We now need reasonable approximations for flow quantities using this ansatz. Klomfass and Muller chose the following:

\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
    u_r (r, \theta) &= \bar{u}_r (r) \cos \theta \\
    u_\theta (r, \theta) &= \bar{u}_\theta (r) \sin \theta \\
    p(r, \theta) &= \bar{p}(r) \cos^2 \theta + p_\infty
\end{aligned}
\label{eq:DRNSE_ansatz}
\end{equation}

Pluggins Eqs. \ref{eq:DRNSE_ansatz} into \ref{eq:polar_div} and setting $f(r) = \rho e_{el-e} + p_e = \rho H_{el-e}$ gives:

\begin{align*}
    \pd{}{x_j} (\rho H_{el-e} u_j) &= \pd{}{r} [\rho H_{el-e} \bar{u}_r \cos \theta] + \frac{2 \rho H_{el-e}}{r} \bar{u}_r \cos \theta + \frac{\cot \theta}{r} \rho H_{el-e} \bar{u}_\theta \sin \theta + \frac{1}{r} \pd{}{\theta} [\rho H_{el-e} \bar{u}_\theta \sin \theta] \\
    &= \bb{ \pd{}{r}[\rho H_{el-e} \bar{u}_r] + \frac{2 \rho H_{el-e}}{r} (\bar{u}_r + \bar{u}_\theta)} \cos \theta \\
\end{align*}

and:

\begin{align*}
    \pd{p_e}{x_j} &= \pd{}{r} [\bar{p}_e \cos^2 \theta + p_e^\infty] + \frac{1}{r} \pd{}{\theta} [\bar{p}_e \cos^2 \theta + p_\infty] \\
    &= \pd{\bar{p}_e}{r} \cos^2 \theta - \frac{1}{r} \bar{p}_e \sin(2 \theta)
\end{align*}

Taking the limit as $\theta$ goes to 0 gives the following identities for stagnation line flow:

\begin{align*}
    \pd{}{x_j} (\rho H_{el-e} u_j) &= \pd{}{r}[\rho H_{el-e} \bar{u}_r] + \frac{2 \rho H_{el-e}}{r} (\bar{u}_r + \bar{u}_\theta) \\
    \pd{p_e}{x_j} &= \pd{p_e}{r}
\end{align*}

The stagnation line equation for the electron-electronic energy is then:

\begin{equation}
    \pd{\rho e_{el-e}}{t} + \pd{}{r}[\rho H_{el-e} u_r] + \frac{2 \rho H_{el-e}}{r} (u_r + u_\theta) + \pd{q_r^{el-e}}{r} + \frac{2}{r} q_r^{el-e} = u_r \pd{p_e}{r} + S_{el-e}
\end{equation}

Where the overbar has been removed for conciseness. In the following sections, all variables are stagnation line quantities and so the overbar is not included again.

\subsection{System of Equations}

The system of equations for the stagnation line solver is:

\begin{equation}
    \pd{\boldsymbol{U}}{t} + \pd{\boldsymbol{F}_c}{r} + \pd{\boldsymbol{F}_d}{r} = \boldsymbol{S}_c + \boldsymbol{S}_d + \boldsymbol{S}_k + \boldsymbol{S}_r
\end{equation}


The conserved variable vector (\textbf{U}), primitive variable vector (\textbf{V}), viscous primitive variable vector (\textbf{W}) and secondary primitive variable vector (\textbf{Y}) are :

\begin{equation*}
    \boldsymbol{U} = \begin{bmatrix}
        \rho_s \\
        \rho u_r \\
        \rho u_\theta \\
        \rho E \\
        \rho e_r \\
        \rho e_v \\
        \rho e_{el-e}
    \end{bmatrix}
    \hspace{5mm} 
    \boldsymbol{V} = \begin{bmatrix}
        \rho_s \\
        u_r \\
        u_\theta \\
        p \\
        e_r \\
        e_v \\ 
        e_{el-e} \\
    \end{bmatrix}
    \hspace{5mm} 
    \boldsymbol{W} = \begin{bmatrix}
        Y_s \\
        u_r \\
        u_\theta \\
        T_{t} \\
        T_{r} \\
        T_{v} \\ 
        T_{e} 
    \end{bmatrix}
    \hspace{5mm} 
    \boldsymbol{Y} = \begin{bmatrix}
        \rho_s \\
        u_r \\
        u_\theta \\
        T_{t} \\
        T_{r} \\
        T_{v} \\
        T_{e}
    \end{bmatrix}
    \hspace{5mm} 
\end{equation*}

The convective flux vector ($\boldsymbol{F}_c$) and diffusive flux vector ($\boldsymbol{F}_d$) are:

\begin{equation}
    \boldsymbol{F_c} = \begin{bmatrix}
        \rho_s u_r\\
        \rho u_r^2 + p \\
        \rho u_r u_\theta \\
        \rho u_r H \\
        \rho u_r e_r \\
        \rho u_r e_v \\
        \rho u_r H_{el-e} 
    \end{bmatrix}
        \hspace{5mm} 
    \boldsymbol{F_d} = \begin{bmatrix}
        J_{rs} \\ 
        \tau_{rr} \\ 
        \tau_{r\theta} \\ 
        q_r + \tau_{rr} u_r \\ 
        q_r^r \\
        q_r^v \\
        q_r^{el-e}
    \end{bmatrix}
\end{equation}

The DRNSE also results in a convective source term ($\boldsymbol{S}_c$) and a diffusive source term ($\boldsymbol{S}_d$), along with the species-mass and vibrational-electronic energy source vector ($\boldsymbol{S}_k$):

\begin{equation}
    \boldsymbol{S}_c = -\frac{2(u_r + u_\theta)}{r}
        \begin{bmatrix}
        \rho_s \\  
        \rho u_r \\ 
        \frac{3}{2} \rho u_\theta - \frac{p-p_\infty}{u_r + u_\theta} \\ 
        \rho H \\ 
        \rho e_r \\
        \rho e_{v} \\
        \rho H_{el-e}
        \end{bmatrix}
    \hspace{5mm}
    \boldsymbol{S}_d = -\frac{2}{r}
        \begin{bmatrix}
        J_{rs} \\ 
        \tau_{rr} - \tau_{\theta \theta} + \tau_{r \theta} \\ 
        \frac{1}{2} (3\tau_{r \theta} - \tau_{\theta \theta}) \\ 
        q_r + \tau_{rr} u_r + \tau_{r \theta} u_r + \tau_{\theta \theta} u_\theta \\
        q_r^r \\
        q_r^v \\
        q_r^{el-e}
        \end{bmatrix}
\end{equation}
\begin{equation}
    \boldsymbol{S}_k =
        \begin{bmatrix}
        \dot{\omega}_s & 0 & 0 & 0 & \mathcal{S}_r & \mathcal{S}_v & \mathcal{S}_{el-e}
        \end{bmatrix}^T
\end{equation}

The terms in the diffusive flux and source vectors are as follows:

\begin{align*}
    \tau_{rr} &= -\frac{4}{3}\mu \bp{\pd{u_r}{r} - \frac{u_r + u_\theta}{r}}
    \\ \tau_{r\theta} &= -\mu \bp{\pd{u_\theta}{r} - \frac{u_r + u_\theta}{r}}
    \\ \tau_{\theta\theta} &= -\frac{1}{2} \tau_{rr}
    \\ q_r &= \sum_s J_{rs} h_s - \kappa^{tr}\pd{T_{tr}}{r} - \kappa^{ve} \pd{T_{ve}}{r}
    \\ q_r^{r} &= \sum_s J_{rs} e_s^{r} - \kappa^{r} \pd{T_{r}}{r}
    \\ q_r^{v} &= \sum_s J_{rs} e_s^{v} - \kappa^{v} \pd{T_{v}}{r}
    \\ q_r^{el-e} &= \sum_s J_{rs} h_s^{el-e} - \kappa^{el-e} \pd{T_{e}}{r}
\end{align*}

The modified Fickian model for the species diffusion flux is used, which is a correction to the standard Fickian model to ensure that the sum of the species diffusion fluxes is zero (i.e., mass conservation is satisfied):
\begin{equation}
    J_{rs} = -\rho D_s \pd{Y_s}{r} + Y_s \sum_{i=1}^\text{NS} \bb{ \rho D_i \pd{Y_i}{r}}
\end{equation}

\subsection{Jacobians}

In this section, we will derive all of the Jacobians that are necessary for the implicit time-stepping of the stagnation line solver. This includes the flux Jacobians, the source term Jacobians, and the Jacobian of the diffusive fluxes. There are quite a number of Jacobians, so a brief summary is given here:

\begin{itemize}
    \item $ \pd{\boldsymbol{U}}{\boldsymbol{V}}$ = Jacobian of conserved variables with respect to primitive variables.
    \item $\pd{\boldsymbol{V}}{\boldsymbol{U}}$ = Jacobian of primitive variables with respect to conserved variables.
    \item $\boldsymbol{N}$ = $\pd{\boldsymbol{U}}{\boldsymbol{Y}}$ = Jacobian of conserved variables with respect to secondary visous primitive variables.
    \item $\boldsymbol{N}^{-1}$ = $\pd{\boldsymbol{Y}}{\boldsymbol{U}}$ = Jacobian of secondary viscous primitive variables with respect to conserved variables.
    \item $\pd{\boldsymbol{F}_c}{\boldsymbol{V}}$ = Jacobian of convective flux vector with respect to primitive variables.
    \item $\tilde{\boldsymbol{R}}$ = Matrix of right eigenvectors of the convective flux Jacobian.
    \item $\tilde{\boldsymbol{L}}$ = Matrix of left eigenvectors of the convective flux Jacobian.
    \item $\boldsymbol{\Lambda}^\pm$ = Diagonal matrix of positive and negative eigenvalues of the convective flux Jacobian.
    \item $\boldsymbol{L}$ = Matrix of left eigenvectors of the convective flux Jacobian multiplied by the Jacobian of the primitive variables with respect to the conserved variables.
    \item $\boldsymbol{R}$ = Matrix of right eigenvectors of the convective flux Jacobian multiplied by the Jacobian of the conserved variables with respect to the primitive variables.
    \item $\boldsymbol{A}^\pm$ = Convective flux Jacobian $\boldsymbol{R} \boldsymbol{\Lambda}^\pm \boldsymbol{L}$.
    \item $\boldsymbol{M}_D^F$ = Coefficient matrix that holds transport quantities for the diffusive flux vector. 
    \item $\boldsymbol{B}_D^F$ = Coefficient vector that holds values not dependant on spatical gradients in the diffusive flux vector.
    \item $\boldsymbol{M}_D^S$ = Coefficient matrix that holds transport quantities for the diffusive source vector.
    \item $\boldsymbol{B}_D^S$ = Coefficient vector that holds values not dependant on spatical gradients in the diffusive source vector.
    \item $\boldsymbol{J}_D = \pd{\boldsymbol{W}}{\boldsymbol{U}}$ = Jacobian matrix  use for mapping from viscous primitive vector to conserved variable vector.
    \item $\boldsymbol{J}_B^F$ = Jacobian of $\boldsymbol{B}_D^F$ with respect to $\boldsymbol{U}$.
    \item $\boldsymbol{J}_B^S$ = Jacobian of $\boldsymbol{B}_D^S$ with respect to $\boldsymbol{U}$.
    \item $\boldsymbol{J}_D^S$ = Jacobian of $\boldsymbol{S}_d$ with respect to $\boldsymbol{U}$.
    \item $\boldsymbol{J}_C^S$ = Jacobian of $\boldsymbol{S}_c$ with respect to $\boldsymbol{U}$.
\end{itemize}

\subsubsection{Convective Flux Jacobians}

In order to find the flux Jacobian $\boldsymbol{A} = \partial{\boldsymbol{F_c}}/\partial{\boldsymbol{U}}$, we need to compute a series of Jacobians. The first is the Jacobian of $\boldsymbol{U}$ with respect to $\boldsymbol{V}$. This is done by putting the conserved variables in terms of the primitive variables and then taking the derivatives. Then for the Jacobians of $\boldsymbol{V}$ with respect to $\boldsymbol{U}$, we do the same. By denoting $\rho_s$ as either $U_s$ or $V_s$, $\rho u_r$ as $U_2$, $u_r$ as $V_2$, etc., we can write the vectors as:

\begin{equation*}
    \boldsymbol{\boldsymbol{V}(\boldsymbol{U})} = \begin{bmatrix}
        U_s \\
        U_2 / \sum_s U_s \\
        U_3 / \sum_s U_s \\
        p(\boldsymbol{U}) \\
        U_5 / \sum_s U_s \\
        U_6 / \sum_s U_s \\
        U_7 / \sum_s U_s 
    \end{bmatrix}
    \hspace{5mm}
    \boldsymbol{\boldsymbol{U}(\boldsymbol{V})} = \begin{bmatrix}
        V_s \\
        \sum_s V_s V_2 \\
        \sum_s V_s V_3 \\
        \rho E(\boldsymbol{V}) \\
        \sum_s V_s V_5 \\
        \sum_s V_s V_6 \\
        \sum_s V_s V_7 
    \end{bmatrix}
    \hspace{5mm}
    \boldsymbol{\boldsymbol{F_c}(\boldsymbol{V})} = \begin{bmatrix}
        V_s V_2 \\
        \sum_s V_s V_2^2 + V_4 \\
        \sum_s V_s V_2 V_3 \\
        [\rho E(\boldsymbol{V}) + V_4]V_2 \\
        \sum_s V_s V_2 V_5 \\
        \sum_s V_s V_2 V_5 \\
        \bb{\sum_{s} V_s V_7 + p_e(\boldsymbol{V})} V_2
    \end{bmatrix}   
\end{equation*}

Pressure is found by using the equation of state for a mixture of ideal gases and recovering translational energy from the total energy equation by subtracting out the other energy modes. For most temperature formulations, the translational and rotational energies are assumed to be in equilibrium with the common temperature $T_{tr}$. For a four temperature model, rotational energy has its own temperature $T_r$. Because of this, some equations have to expressions to account for the difference between these models. Equations highlighted in black are equations that specifically account for four temperatures, while equations in $\textcolor{defgreen}{green}$ assume translational-rotational equilibrium. The pressure is split up between contributions from the heavy-species and the electons:

\begin{equation*}
    p = \sum_{s}^\text{NH} \rho_s R_s T_t + \rho_e R_e T_e = p_h + p_e
\end{equation*}

In conservative variable form, this is:

\begin{equation*}
    p = \frac{\displaystyle \sum_s^\text{NH} \rho_s R_s }{\displaystyle \sum_s^\text{NH} \rho_s c_{v,s}^t} \bb{\rho E - \sum_i E_i} + \rho _e \frac{R_e}{c_{v,e}^t} T_e
\end{equation*}

Where the sum of $E_i$ is taken over all internal energy modes along the kinetic energy and heat of formation. This is equivalent to:

\begin{equation*}
    \sum_\text{i} E_i = \Erot + \Evib + \Eele + \Eke + \Eform
\end{equation*}

Therefore, in terms of conserved variables for Jacobians, the pressure is:

\begin{equation*}
    p = \frac{\displaystyle \sum_s^\text{NH} U_s R_s }{\displaystyle \sum_s^\text{NH} U_s c_{v,s}^t} \bb{U_4 - U_5 - U_6 - U_7 - \sum_s^\text{NS} U_s h_{f,s}^\circ - \frac{1}{2} \frac{U_2^2}{\sum_s^\text{NS} U_s}} + \rho_e \frac{R_e}{c_{v,e}^t} T_e
\end{equation*}

Internal energy can be expressed using primitive variables by rearranging the equation for pressure:

\begin{align*}
